# Gaza Strip

Not to be confused with [Gaza Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaza_Province) or [Gaza Governorate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaza_Governorate).

The **Gaza Strip**, also known simply as **Gaza**,[^cite_note-10] is the smaller of the two Palestinian territories (the other being the [West Bank](33209.md)) that make up the [State of Palestine](241405.md) in the [Southern Levant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Levant) region of [West Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Asia). Gaza is bordered by [Egypt](8087628.md) on the southwest, [Israel](9282173.md) on the east and north, and the [Mediterranean Sea](19006.md) to the west. Its capital and largest city is [Gaza City](55387.md).[^cite_note-11]&#x20;

The territorial boundaries were established by the [Green Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_Line_\(Israel\)) of the [1949 Armistice Agreements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1949_Armistice_Agreements) at the conclusion of the [1948 Palestine war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1948_Palestine_war), at which time Gaza was [controlled](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All-Palestine_Protectorate) by the [Kingdom of Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Egypt). During that period, the [All-Palestine Protectorate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All-Palestine_Protectorate), also known as All-Palestine, was established with limited recognition, and it became a refuge for [Palestinians](23267.md) who [fled or were expelled](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1948_Palestinian_expulsion_and_flight) by Zionist forces in the [Nakba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nakba).[^cite_note-fm-12][^cite_note-il-13] In the [1967 Arab–Israeli War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1967_Arab%E2%80%93Israeli_War), Israel captured and [occupied the Gaza Strip](76465951.md) among other territories, initiating a decades-long military [occupation of the Palestinian territories](2781576.md).[^cite_note-fm-12][^cite_note-il-13] The mid-1990s [Oslo Accords](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oslo_Accords) established the [Palestinian Authority](24093.md) (PA) as a limited governing authority, initially led by the [secular](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secular) party [Fatah](11104.md) until its [electoral defeat in 2006](38945340.md) to the [Sunni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni) [Islamist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni_Islamism) [Hamas](13913.md). Hamas would then take over the [governance of Gaza](38626632.md) in the [Battle of Gaza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Gaza_\(2007\)) the next year,[^cite_note-rp-14][^cite_note-15] subsequently warring with Israel.&#x20;

The restrictions on movement and goods in Gaza imposed by Israel date back to the early 1990s.[^cite_note-16] In 2005, Israel [unilaterally withdrew its military forces from Gaza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_disengagement_from_the_Gaza_Strip), dismantled [its settlements](15123.md), and implemented a temporary [blockade of Gaza](19224310.md).[^cite_note-be-17] The blockade became indefinite after the 2007 Hamas takeover.[^cite_note-eb-18][^cite_note-be-17] Egypt also began its blockade of Gaza in 2007. Despite the Israeli disengagement, Gaza was still considered occupied by Israel under [international law](8195726.md),[^cite_note-occ-19][^cite_note-20] being described as an "[open-air prison](#Characterization_as_open-air_prison)".[^cite_note-21][^cite_note-open-air_prison-22] Israel's actions in Gaza since the start of the [war that began in 2023](74998519.md) have resulted in large-scale loss of life, [mass population displacement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaza_Strip_evacuations), a [humanitarian crisis](75277791.md), and an [ongoing famine](75828824.md).[^cite_note-Dannenbaum_Dill_2024-23][^cite_note-IPC_22_August_2025-24] These actions have been described by scholars, international law experts, and human-rights organizations as constituting [a genocide against the Palestinian people](75668427.md).[^cite_note-genocide-25][^cite_note-genocide_int_law_scholars-26] [A provisional ceasefire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/January_2025_Gaza_war_ceasefire) began in mid-January 2025, lasting two months. In August, Israel began [an offensive on Gaza City](80801475.md). By 9 October 2025, Israel and Hamas agreed to the first part of [a United States-led peace plan](81215079.md). [United Nations Security Council Resolution 2803](81623997.md), adopted on 17 November 2025, contains provisions for the [transitional governance of the Gaza Strip](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaza_Strip_under_Resolution_2803).&#x20;

The Gaza Strip is 41 kilometres (25 miles) long, from 6 to 12 km (3.7 to 7.5 mi) wide, and has a total area of 365 km<sup>2</sup> (141 sq mi).[^cite_note-Arnon-27][^cite_note-CIA_World_Factbook-28] As of 2010, it has a population of just over 2 million, mostly of [Palestinian refugees](74681.md) and their descendants. It is one of the [most densely populated territories in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_and_dependencies_by_population_density), with a population density similar to that of cities like [Tel Aviv](31453.md) or [London](17867.md). Before the Gaza war, its capital Gaza City was roughly the 90th-[most densely populated city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_proper_by_population_density) in the world, and had about a third of the population density of cities like [Malé](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mal%C3%A9) or [Giza](144514.md).[^cite_note-29] The Gaza Strip has a high proportion of youth, with 43.5% being children 14 or younger and 50% under age of 18.[^cite_note-Norman_G._Finkelstein-30] Sunni Islam is almost ubiquitous, with [a Palestinian Christian minority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gazan_Christians). Gaza has an annual population growth rate of 1.99% (2023 est.), the [39th-highest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_population_growth_rate) in the world.[^cite_note-CIA_World_Factbook-28] Gaza's unemployment rate is among the highest in the world, with an overall unemployment rate of 46% and a youth unemployment rate of 70%.[^cite_note-eb-18][^cite_note-31] Despite this, the [area's 97% literacy rate is higher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_literacy_rate) than that of nearby Egypt, while youth literacy is 88%.[^cite_note-od-32] Gaza has throughout the years been seen as a source of [Palestinian nationalism](47246228.md) and resistance.[^cite_note-Sara_M._Roy-33][^cite_note-34][^cite_note-35]&#x20;

## Etymology

This section is an excerpt from [Gaza City § Etymology](55387.md#Etymology).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Gaza_City\&action=edit)]

The name *Gaza* first appears in the military records of [Thutmose III](155730.md) of [New Kingdom of Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Kingdom_of_Egypt) in the 15th century BC,[^cite_note-36] and was mentioned in the [Amarna correspondence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amarna_correspondence) as *Āl Ḫazzati* and other variant spellings.[^cite_note-37] In [Neo-Assyrian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-Assyrian) sources, reflecting the late [Philistine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philistine) period, it was known as *Ḫāzat*.[^cite_note-38] It is clear that the name originates from none of these languages, however.&#x20;

Based on the city's [Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_language) name, עַזָּה *ʿAzzā*, a common [folk etymology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/False_etymology) insists the name stems from the [Semitic root](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semitic_root) *[ʿayin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%CA%BFayin)*-*[zayin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zayin)*-*zayin*, from which words related to strength and fierceness are derived,[^cite_note-Gaza_City_Masalha-39] but this is unlikely. The in the root corresponds to a [Proto-Semitic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proto-Semitic) \**ʿ* (/[ʕ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voiced_pharyngeal_fricative)/) sound (compare Hebrew עַז *ʿaz* with [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic) عَزَّ *ʿazza*, both meaning "to be strong, powerful, mighty"), while it is clear from city's name in Arabic (غَزَّة, *Ḡazza*), [Koine Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koine_Greek) (Γάζα, *Gắză*), and [Egyptian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_language) (*gꜣḏꜣtw*) that the name of Gaza was likely originally pronounced with an initial \**ḡ* (/[ɣ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voiced_velar_fricative)/\~/[ʁ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voiced_uvular_fricative)/) sound, and thus cannot have been from the same root as עַז *ʿaz*.&#x20;

Historically, Muslims often referred to the city as *Ḡazzat Hāšim* in honor of [Hashim ibn Abd Manaf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hashim_ibn_Abd_Manaf), the great-grandfather of [Muhammad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad) who, according to [Islamic tradition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_mythology), is buried in the city.[^cite_note-Gaza_City_Shahinp414-40]&#x20;

## History

Main article: [History of the Gaza Strip](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Gaza_Strip)

See also: [History of Gaza City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Gaza)

### Before 1948

Historically part of the [Palestine region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestine_region), the area was controlled since the 16th century by the [Ottoman Empire](22278.md); in 1906, the Ottomans and the [British Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire) set [the region's international border](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egypt%E2%80%93Gaza_border) with [Egypt](8087628.md).[^cite_note-tn-41] With the defeat of the [Central Powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Powers) in [World War I](4764461.md) and the subsequent [partition of the Ottoman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partition_of_the_Ottoman_Empire), the British offered the governance of the Gaza Strip area to Egypt, which declined the responsibility.[^cite_note-42] Britain itself kept and ruled the territory it had occupied in 1917–18, from 1920 until 1948, under the internationally accepted framework of "[Mandatory Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandatory_Palestine)". Villages along the Gaza coastal plain expanded significantly during this period, with both demographic increase and [intensified agricultural production](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_dune_agriculture).[^cite_note-43][^cite_note-44]&#x20;

### &#x20;1948–1959: All-Palestine government

Main articles: [All-Palestine Protectorate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All-Palestine_Protectorate) and [All-Palestine Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All-Palestine_Government)



Palestine refugees initially displaced to [Beach camp](16296150.md) in Gaza board boats to Lebanon or Egypt during the 1948 war



Palestine refugees leave the Gaza Strip on fishing boats in the wake of the war in 1948

During the [1948 Palestine war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1948_Palestine_war) and more specifically the [1948 Arab–Israeli War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1948_Arab%E2%80%93Israeli_War), tens of thousands of [Palestinian refugees](74681.md) [fled or were expelled](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1948_Palestinian_expulsion_and_flight) to the Gaza Strip.[^cite_note-Reuters-45] By the end of the war, 25% of Mandatory Palestine's [Arab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab) population was in Gaza, though the Strip constituted only 1% of the land.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFiliu201471-72-46] The same year, the [United Nations](31769.md) Relief and Works Agency for Palestine Refugees in the Near East ([UNRWA](32270.md)) was established to administer various refugee programmes.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFiliu201476-47]&#x20;

On 22 September 1948 (near the end of the Arab–Israeli War), in the Egyptian-occupied [Gaza City](55387.md), the [Arab League](52625.md) proclaimed the All-Palestine Government, partly to limit [Transjordan](7515964.md)'s influence over Palestine. The All-Palestine Protectorate was quickly recognized by six of the Arab League's then-seven members (excluding Transjordan): Egypt, [Syria](7515849.md), [Lebanon](17771.md), [Iraq](7515928.md), [Saudi Arabia](349303.md), and [Yemen](350939.md).[^cite_note-48][^cite_note-49]&#x20;

After the cessation of hostilities, the [Israel–Egypt Armistice Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel%E2%80%93Egypt_Armistice_Agreement) of 24 February 1949 established the line of separation between Egyptian and Israeli forces, as well as the modern boundary between Gaza and Israel, which both signatories declared not to be an international border. The southern border with Egypt was unchanged.[^cite_note-50][^cite_note-51][^cite_note-tn-41]&#x20;

Palestinians living in Gaza or Egypt were issued All-Palestine passports. Egypt did not offer them citizenship. From the end of 1949, they received aid directly from UNRWA. During the [Suez Crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suez_Crisis) (1956), Gaza and the [Sinai Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinai_Peninsula) were occupied by Israeli troops, who withdrew under international pressure. The All-Palestine government was accused of being little more than a façade for Egyptian control, with negligible independent funding or influence. It subsequently moved to [Cairo](6293.md) and dissolved in 1959 by decree of Egyptian president [Gamal Abdul Nasser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gamal_Abdul_Nasser).[^cite_note-52]&#x20;

### 1956–1957: Israeli occupation

Main article: [Israeli occupation of the Gaza Strip](76465951.md)

Further information: [Suez Crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suez_Crisis)



Palestinians in an outdoor market in the Gaza Strip in 1956

During the 1956 Suez Crisis (the Second Arab–Israeli war), Israel invaded Gaza and the Sinai Peninsula. On 3 November, the [Israel Defense Forces](39237.md) (IDF) attacked Egyptian and Palestinian forces at [Khan Yunis](101330.md).[^cite_note-Varble,_Derek,_p._45-53] The city of Khan Yunis resisted being captured, and Israel responded with a heavy bombing campaign that inflicted heavy civilian casualties.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFiliu201496-54] After a fierce battle, the Israeli 37th Armored Brigade's [Sherman tanks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M4_Sherman) broke through the heavily fortified lines outside of Khan Yunis held by the 86th Palestinian Brigade.[^cite_note-Varble,_Derek,_p._46-55]&#x20;

After some street-fighting with Egyptian soldiers and [Palestinian fedayeen](14068131.md), Khan Yunis fell to the Israelis.[^cite_note-Varble,_Derek,_p._46-55] Upon capturing Khan Yunis, the IDF committed [an alleged massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khan_Yunis_massacre).[^cite_note-lat3-56] Israeli troops started executing unarmed Palestinians, mostly civilians; in one instance men were lined up against walls in central square and executed with [machine guns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Machine_gun).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFiliu201497-57] The claims of a massacre were reported to the [UN General Assembly](31957.md) on 15 December 1956 by UNRWA director [Henry Labouisse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Richardson_Labouisse_Jr.), who reported from "trustworthy sources" that 275 people were killed in the massacre, of which 140 were refugees and 135 local residents.[^cite_note-58][^cite_note-Sacco2-59]&#x20;

On 12 November, days after the hostilities had ended, Israel [killed 111 people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1956_Rafah_massacre) in the [Rafah refugee camp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rafah_refugee_camp) during Israeli operations, provoking international criticism.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFiliu201499-100-60][^cite_note-lat3-56]&#x20;

Israel ended the occupation in March 1957, amid international pressure. During the four-month Israeli occupation, 900–1,231 people were killed.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFiliu2014105-61] According to French historian [Jean-Pierre Filiu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Pierre_Filiu), 1% of the population of Gaza was killed, wounded, imprisoned or tortured during the occupation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFiliu2014105-61]&#x20;

### 1959–1967: Egyptian occupation

Main article: [Occupation of the Gaza Strip by Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occupation_of_the_Gaza_Strip_by_Egypt)



[Che Guevara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Che_Guevara) visiting Gaza in 1959

After the dissolution of the All-Palestine Government in 1959, under the excuse of pan-Arabism, Egypt continued to occupy Gaza until 1967. Egypt never annexed the Strip, but instead treated it as a controlled territory and administered it through a military governor.[^cite_note-62] The influx of over 200,000 refugees from former Mandatory Palestine, roughly a quarter of [those who fled or were expelled from their homes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_refugee) during, and in the aftermath of, the 1948 Arab–Israeli War into Gaza[^cite_note-63] resulted in a dramatic decrease in the standard of living. Because the Egyptian government restricted movement to and from Gaza, its inhabitants could not look elsewhere for gainful employment.[^cite_note-JB-64]&#x20;

### 1967: Israeli occupation

Main article: [Israeli occupation of the Gaza Strip](76465951.md)

See also: [Israeli Military Governorate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_Military_Governorate), [Israeli Civil Administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_Civil_Administration), and [1967–71 Gazan insurgency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1967%E2%80%9371_Gazan_insurgency)

In June 1967, during the Six-Day War, IDF captured Gaza. Under the then head of Israel's Southern Command [Ariel Sharon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ariel_Sharon), dozens of Palestinians, suspected of being members of the resistance, were executed without trial.[^cite_note-65]&#x20;

Between 1967 and 1968, Israel evicted approximately 75,000 residents of the Gaza Strip who Golda Meir described as a "fifth column". In addition, at least 25,000 Gazan residents were prevented from returning after the 1967 war. Ultimately, the Strip lost 25% (a conservative estimate) of its prewar population between 1967 and 1968.[^cite_note-66] In 1970–1971 [Ariel Sharon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ariel_Sharon) implemented what became known as a 'five finger' strategy, which consisted in creating military areas and settlements by breaking the Strip into five zones to better enable Israeli occupation, settlement and, by discontinuous fragmentation of the Palestinian zones created, allow an efficient management of the area. Thousands of homes were bulldozed and large numbers of Bedouin families were exiled to the Sinai.[^cite_note-67][^cite_note-68][^cite_note-69]&#x20;

Between 1973 (after the [Yom Kippur War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yom_Kippur_War)) and 1987, official policy on economic development in the Gaza Strip remained the same as in 1969 with limited local investment and economic opportunity coming primarily from employment in Israel.[^cite_note-dd-70]&#x20;



Gaza City in 1967

According to [Tom Segev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tom_Segev), moving the Palestinians out of the country had been a persistent element of Zionist thinking from early times.[^cite_note-TSegev-71] In December 1967, during a meeting at which the [Security Cabinet](22256400.md) [brainstormed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brainstorming) about what to do with the Arab population of the newly occupied territories, one of the suggestions Prime Minister [Levi Eshkol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levi_Eshkol) proffered regarding Gaza was that the people might leave if Israel restricted their access to water supplies.[^cite_note-72] A number of measures, including financial incentives, were taken shortly afterwards to begin to encourage Gazans to emigrate elsewhere.[^cite_note-TSegev-71][^cite_note-73] Following the 1967 Arab-Israeli War, "various international agencies struggled to respond" and [American Near East Refugee Aid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Near_East_Refugee_Aid) was founded to help victims of the conflict by providing immediate emergency relief.[^cite_note-74]&#x20;



Israeli soldiers in Gaza in 1969

Subsequent to this military victory, Israel created the first [Israeli settlement](15123.md) bloc in the Strip, [Gush Katif](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gush_Katif), in a spot where a [small kibbutz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kfar_Darom) had previously existed for 18 months between 1946 and 1948. The kibbutz community had been established as part of the [Jewish Agency's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_Agency_for_Israel) "[11 points in the Negev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/11_points_in_the_Negev)" plan, in which 11 Jewish villages were built across the Negev in a single night as a response to the [Morrison-Grady Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morrison-Grady_Plan), which threatened to exclude the Negev from a future Jewish State. In total, between 1967 and 2005, Israel established 21 settlements in Gaza, comprising 20% of the total territory. The [economic growth rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gross_national_product) from 1967 to 1982 averaged roughly 9.7% per annum, due in good part to expanded income from work opportunities inside Israel, which had a major utility for the latter by supplying the country with a large unskilled and semi-skilled workforce. Gaza's agricultural sector was adversely affected as one-third of the Strip was appropriated by Israel, competition for scarce water resources stiffened, and the lucrative cultivation of citrus declined with the advent of Israeli policies, such as prohibitions on planting new trees and taxation that gave breaks to Israeli producers, factors which militated against growth. Gaza's direct exports of these products to Western markets, as opposed to Arab markets, was prohibited except through Israeli marketing vehicles, in order to assist Israeli citrus exports to the same markets. The overall result was that large numbers of farmers were forced out of the agricultural sector. Israel placed quotas on all goods exported from Gaza, while abolishing restrictions on the flow of Israeli goods into the Strip. [Sara Roy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sara_Roy) characterised the pattern as one of structural de-development.[^cite_note-dd-70]&#x20;

On 26 March 1979, Israel and Egypt signed the [Egypt–Israel peace treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egypt%E2%80%93Israel_peace_treaty).[^cite_note-75] Among other things, the treaty provided for the withdrawal by Israel of its armed forces and civilians from the Sinai Peninsula, which Israel had captured during the Six-Day War. The Egyptians agreed to keep the Sinai Peninsula demilitarized. The final status of the Gaza Strip, and other relations between Israel and Palestinians, was not dealt with in the treaty. Egypt renounced all territorial claims to territory north of the international border. The Gaza Strip remained under Israeli military administration. The Israeli military became responsible for the maintenance of civil facilities and services.&#x20;

After the 1979 Egypt–Israel peace treaty, a 100-meter-wide [buffer zone](489870.md) between Gaza and Egypt known as the [Philadelphi Route](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphi_Route) was established. The international border along the [Philadelphi corridor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphi_corridor) between Egypt and Gaza is 11 kilometres (7 miles) long.&#x20;

### 1987: First Intifada

Main article: [First Intifada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Intifada)



Israeli soldiers opposite Palestinian protesters in the strip during the [First Intifada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Intifada) in 1987

The First Intifada was a sustained series of protests and violent riots carried out by [Palestinians](23267.md) in the [Israeli-occupied](2781576.md) [Palestinian territories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_territories) and Israel.[^cite_note-76] It was motivated by collective Palestinian frustration over Israel's military occupation of the [West Bank](33209.md) and the Gaza Strip, as it approached a twenty-year mark, having begun after Israel's victory in the [1967 Arab–Israeli War](29328.md).[^cite_note-LockmanBeinin1989_5-77] The uprising lasted from December 1987 until the [Madrid Conference of 1991](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madrid_Conference_of_1991), though some date its conclusion to 1993, with the signing of the [Oslo Accords](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oslo_Accords).&#x20;

The intifada began on 9 December 1987,[^cite_note-LockmanBeinin1989_5-77] in the [Jabalia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jabalia_Camp) [refugee camp](438046.md) of the Gaza Strip after an Israeli army truck collided with a civilian car, killing four Palestinian workers.[^cite_note-OmerMan2001-78] Palestinians charged that the collision was a deliberate response for the killing of an Israeli in Gaza days earlier.[^cite_note-79] Israel denied that the crash, which came at time of heightened tensions, was intentional or coordinated.[^cite_note-80] The Palestinian response was characterized by protests, [civil disobedience](37073.md), and violence.[^cite_note-books.google.com-81][^cite_note-Lustick_1993_560–594-82] There was [graffiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Graffiti), [barricading](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barricade),[^cite_note-BBC_NEWS-83][^cite_note-Walid_Salem_2005,_pp._179-84] and widespread [throwing of stones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_stone-throwing) and [Molotov cocktails](20922.md) at the IDF and its infrastructure within the West Bank and Gaza Strip. These contrasted with civil efforts including [general strikes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_strikes), [boycotts](18591996.md) of [Israeli Civil Administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_Civil_Administration) institutions in the Gaza Strip and the West Bank, an economic [boycott](18591996.md) consisting of refusal to work in Israeli settlements on Israeli products, refusal to pay taxes, and refusal to drive Palestinian cars with Israeli licenses.[^cite_note-books.google.com-81][^cite_note-Lustick_1993_560–594-82][^cite_note-BBC_NEWS-83]&#x20;

### 1994: Gaza under Palestinian Authority

In May 1994, following the Palestinian-Israeli agreements known as the Oslo Accords, a phased transfer of governmental authority to the Palestinians took place. Much of the Strip came under Palestinian control, except for the settlement blocs and military areas. The Israeli forces left Gaza City and other urban areas, leaving the new Palestinian Authority to administer and police those areas. The Palestinian Authority, led by [Yasser Arafat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yasser_Arafat), chose Gaza City as its first provincial headquarters. In September 1995, Israel and the [Palestine Liberation Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestine_Liberation_Organization) (PLO) signed [a second agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interim_Agreement_on_the_West_Bank_and_the_Gaza_Strip), extending the Palestinian Authority to most West Bank towns.&#x20;

Between 1994 and 1996, Israel built the [Gaza–Israel barrier](478337.md) to improve security in Israel. The barrier was largely torn down by Palestinians at the beginning of the [Second Intifada](71717.md) in September 2000.[^cite_note-Doron_Almog-85]&#x20;

### 2000: Second Intifada

Main article: [Second Intifada](71717.md)



An [IDF Caterpillar D9](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IDF_Caterpillar_D9) [armored bulldozer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armored_bulldozer) demolishing a house in the Gaza Strip during the Second Intifada

The Second Intifada was a major [Palestinian](23267.md) uprising in the [Israeli-occupied](2781576.md) [Palestinian territories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_territories) and Israel. The general triggers for the unrest are speculated to have been centred on the failure of the [2000 Camp David Summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000_Camp_David_Summit), which was expected to reach a final agreement on the [Israeli–Palestinian peace process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli%E2%80%93Palestinian_peace_process) in July 2000.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPressman2003114-86] Outbreaks of violence began in September 2000, after [Ariel Sharon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ariel_Sharon), then the [Israeli opposition leader](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leader_of_the_Opposition_\(Israel\)), made a provocative visit to the [Al-Aqsa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Aqsa) compound on the [Temple Mount](65719.md) in [Jerusalem](16043.md);[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPressman2003114-86] the visit itself was peaceful, but, as anticipated, sparked protests and riots that Israeli police put down with rubber bullets and tear gas.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEByman2011114-87] The Second Intifada also marked the beginning of rocket attacks and bombings of Israeli border localities by Palestinian guerrillas from the Gaza Strip, especially by the [Hamas](13913.md) and [Palestinian Islamic Jihad](13093253.md) movements.&#x20;

High numbers of casualties were caused among civilians as well as combatants. Israeli forces engaged in gunfire, [targeted killings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Targeted_killings_by_the_Israel_Defense_Forces), and tank and aerial attacks, while Palestinians engaged in [suicide bombings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Palestinian_suicide_attacks), gunfire, [stone-throwing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_stone-throwing), and [rocket attacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_rocket_attacks_on_Israel).[^cite_note-88][^cite_note-89] Palestinian suicide bombings were a prominent feature of the fighting and mainly targeted Israeli civilians, contrasting with the relatively less violent nature of the [First Intifada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Intifada).[^cite_note-NMRR-90][^cite_note-91] With a combined casualty figure for combatants and civilians, the violence is estimated to have resulted in the deaths of approximately 3,000 Palestinians and 1,000 Israelis, as well as 64 foreigners.[^cite_note-92]&#x20;

Between December 2000 and June 2001, the barrier between Gaza and Israel was reconstructed. A barrier on the Gaza Strip-Egypt border was constructed starting in 2004.[^cite_note-Anne_Barnard-93] The main crossing points are the northern [Erez Crossing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erez_Crossing) into Israel and the southern [Rafah Crossing](10475618.md) into Egypt. The eastern [Karni Crossing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karni_crossing) used for cargo, closed down in 2011.[^cite_note-Greg_Myre-94] Israel controls the Gaza Strip's northern borders, as well as its territorial waters and airspace. Egypt controls Gaza Strip's southern border, under an agreement between it and Israel.[^cite_note-BBC-95] Neither Israel or Egypt permits free travel from Gaza as both borders are heavily militarily fortified. "Egypt maintains a strict blockade on Gaza in order to isolate Hamas from Islamist insurgents in the Sinai."[^cite_note-96]&#x20;

### 2005: Israel's unilateral disengagement

Main article: [Israeli disengagement from Gaza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_disengagement_from_Gaza)



[Neve Dekalim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neve_Dekalim) was [Gush Katif](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gush_Katif)'s urban center and its largest community

In 2005, Israel withdrew from the Gaza Strip and dismantled its settlements.[^cite_note-gs-97] Israel also withdrew from the [Philadelphi Route](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphi_Route), a narrow strip of land adjacent to the border with Egypt, after Egypt agreed to secure its side of the border after the [Agreement on Movement and Access](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agreement_on_Movement_and_Access), known as the Rafah Agreement.[^cite_note-98] The Gaza Strip was left under the control of the [Palestinian Authority](24093.md).[^cite_note-99]&#x20;

### Post-2006: Hamas takeover

Main article: [Fatah–Hamas conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatah%E2%80%93Hamas_conflict)

In the [Palestinian parliamentary elections](38945340.md) held on 25 January 2006, Hamas won a plurality of 42.9% of the total vote and 74 out of 132 total seats (56%).[^cite_note-100][^cite_note-101] When Hamas assumed power the next month, Israel, the United States, the EU, Russia and the UN demanded that Hamas accept all previous agreements, recognize Israel's right to exist, and renounce violence; when Hamas refused,[^cite_note-102] they [cut off direct aid to the Palestinian Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006%E2%80%9307_economic_sanctions_against_the_Palestinian_National_Authority), although some aid money was redirected to humanitarian organizations not affiliated with the government.[^cite_note-103] The resulting political disorder and economic stagnation led to many Palestinians emigrating from the Gaza Strip.[^cite_note-104]&#x20;



[Gaza City](55387.md) skyline, 2007

In January 2007, fighting erupted between Hamas and [Fatah](11104.md). The deadliest clashes occurred in the northern Gaza Strip. On 30 January 2007, a truce was negotiated between Fatah and Hamas.[^cite_note-105] After a few days, new fighting broke out. On 1 February, Hamas killed 6 people in an ambush on a Gaza convoy which delivered equipment for Abbas' [Palestinian Presidential Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_Presidential_Guard).[^cite_note-106] Fatah fighters stormed a Hamas-affiliated university in the Gaza Strip. Officers from Abbas' presidential guard battled Hamas gunmen guarding the Hamas-led Interior Ministry.[^cite_note-107] In May 2007, new fighting broke out between the factions.[^cite_note-108] Interior Minister [Hani Qawasmi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talab_al-Qawasmi), who had been considered a moderate civil servant acceptable to both factions, resigned due to what he termed harmful behavior by both sides.[^cite_note-109]&#x20;

Fighting spread in the Gaza Strip, with both factions attacking vehicles and facilities of the other side. Following a breakdown in an Egyptian-brokered truce, Israel launched an air strike which destroyed a building used by Hamas. Ongoing violence prompted fear that it could bring the end of the [Fatah-Hamas coalition government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_government_of_March_2007), and possibly the end of the Palestinian authority.[^cite_note-110] Hamas spokesman [Mousa Abu Marzook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mousa_Abu_Marzook) blamed the conflict between Hamas and Fatah on Israel, stating that the constant pressure of economic sanctions resulted in the "real explosion."[^cite_note-111] From 2006 to 2007 more than 600 Palestinians were killed in fighting between Hamas and Fatah.[^cite_note-112] 349 Palestinians were killed in fighting between factions in 2007. 160 Palestinians killed each other in June alone.[^cite_note-113]&#x20;

### 2007: Fatah–Hamas conflict

Main articles: [Battle of Gaza (2007)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Gaza_\(2007\)) and [Fatah–Hamas conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatah%E2%80%93Hamas_conflict)



Gaza City in 2007

Following the victory of Hamas in the [2006 Palestinian legislative election](38945340.md), Hamas and [Fatah](11104.md) formed the [Palestinian authority national unity government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_authority_national_unity_government) headed by [Ismail Haniyeh](41207463.md). Shortly after, Hamas took control of the Gaza Strip in the course of the Battle of Gaza (June 2007),[^cite_note-Guardian-114] seizing government institutions and replacing Fatah and other government officials with its own.[^cite_note-intern_fight_p14-15-115] By 14 June, Hamas fully controlled the Gaza Strip. [Palestinian president](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_the_Palestinian_National_Authority) [Mahmoud Abbas](232595.md) responded by declaring a [state of emergency](298608.md), dissolving the unity government and forming a new government without Hamas participation. [PNA security forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Preventive_Security_Service) in the [West Bank](33209.md) arrested a number of Hamas members.&#x20;

In late June 2008, Egypt, Saudi Arabia and Jordan declared the West Bank-based cabinet formed by Abbas as "the sole legitimate Palestinian government". Egypt moved its embassy from Gaza to the West Bank.[^cite_note-116] [Saudi Arabia](349303.md) and Egypt supported reconciliation and a new unity government and pressed Abbas to start talks with Hamas. Abbas had always conditioned this on Hamas returning control of the Gaza Strip to the Palestinian Authority. After the takeover, Israel and Egypt [closed their border crossings with Gaza](19224310.md). Palestinian sources reported that European Union monitors fled the [Rafah Border Crossing](10475618.md), on the Gaza–Egypt border for fear of being kidnapped or harmed.[^cite_note-EU_flee_Rafah_crossing-117] Arab foreign ministers and Palestinian officials presented a united front against control of the border by Hamas.[^cite_note-Abbas_wins_Egypt_backing_on_border-118] Meanwhile, Israeli and Egyptian security reports said that Hamas continued smuggling in large quantities of explosives and arms from Egypt through tunnels. Egyptian security forces uncovered 60 tunnels in 2007.[^cite_note-119]&#x20;

#### Egyptian border barrier breach



Gaza in January 2009

On 23 January 2008, after months of preparation during which the steel reinforcement of the border barrier was weakened,[^cite_note-120] [Hamas destroyed several parts of the wall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breach_of_the_Gaza-Egypt_border_\(2008\)) dividing Gaza and Egypt in the town of [Rafah](584373.md). Hundreds of thousands of Gazans crossed the border into Egypt seeking food and supplies. Due to the crisis, Egyptian president [Hosni Mubarak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hosni_Mubarak) ordered his troops to allow the Palestinians in but to verify that they did not bring weapons back across the border.[^cite_note-121] Egypt arrested and later released several armed Hamas militants in the Sinai who presumably wanted to infiltrate into Israel. At the same time, Israel increased its state of alert along the length of the Israel–Egypt Sinai border, and warned its citizens to leave Sinai "without delay."&#x20;

In February 2008, the [Gaza–Israel conflict](16346266.md) intensified, with rockets launched at Israeli cities. Aggression by Hamas led to [Israeli military action](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Hot_Winter) on 29 February 2008, resulting in over 110 Palestinians being killed according to BBC News, as well as two Israeli soldiers. Israeli human rights group [B'Tselem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/B%27Tselem) estimated that 45 of those killed were not involved in hostilities, and 15 were minors.[^cite_note-122]&#x20;

### 2008–2009: Gaza War

Main article: [Gaza War (2008–2009)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaza_War_\(2008%E2%80%932009\))



Buildings damaged during the 2008-2009 Gaza war

On 27 December 2008,[^cite_note-123] Israeli [F-16](11642.md) fighters launched a series of air strikes against targets in Gaza following the breakdown of a temporary truce between Israel and Hamas.[^cite_note-124] Israel began a ground invasion of the Gaza Strip on 3 January 2009.[^cite_note-125] Various sites that Israel claimed were being used as weapons depots were struck from the air : police stations, schools, hospitals, UN warehouses, mosques, various Hamas government buildings and other buildings.[^cite_note-126]&#x20;

Israel said that the attack was a response to Hamas rocket attacks on southern Israel, which totaled [over 3,000 in 2008](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Palestinian_rocket_attacks_on_Israel,_2008), and which intensified during the few weeks preceding the operation. Israel advised people near military targets to leave before the attacks. Israeli defense sources said that Defense Minister [Ehud Barak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ehud_Barak) instructed the IDF to prepare for the operation six months before it began, using long-term planning and intelligence-gathering.[^cite_note-127]&#x20;



Gaza City in 2012

A total of 1,100–1,400[^cite_note-128] Palestinians (295–926 civilians) and 13 Israelis were killed in the 22-day war.[^cite_note-129] The conflict damaged or destroyed tens of thousands of homes,[^cite_note-130][^cite_note-131] 15 of Gaza's 27 hospitals and 43 of its 110 primary health care facilities,[^cite_note-132] 800 water wells,[^cite_note-133] 186 greenhouses,[^cite_note-134] and nearly all of its 10,000 family farms;[^cite_note-135] leaving 50,000 homeless,[^cite_note-news.bbc.co.uk-136] 400,000–500,000 without running water,[^cite_note-news.bbc.co.uk-136][^cite_note-Gaza:_Humanitarian_situation-137] one million without electricity,[^cite_note-Gaza:_Humanitarian_situation-137] and resulting in acute food shortages.[^cite_note-138] The people of Gaza still suffer from the loss of these facilities and homes, especially since they have great challenges to rebuild them.&#x20;

### 2014: Gaza War

On 5 June 2014, Fatah signed a unity agreement with the Hamas political party.[^cite_note-bbc.com-139]&#x20;

The [2014 Gaza War](43243199.md), also known as Operation Protective Edge, was a military operation launched by Israel on 8 July 2014 in the Gaza Strip. Following the [kidnapping and murder of three Israeli teenagers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_kidnapping_and_murder_of_Israeli_teenagers) in the [West Bank](33209.md) by Hamas-affiliated [Palestinian](23267.md) militants, the IDF initiated *Operation Brother's Keeper*, in which some 350 Palestinians, including nearly all of the active Hamas militants in the West Bank, were arrested.[^cite_note-nathanthrall-140][^cite_note-HamasAcc-141][^cite_note-HamasAcc2-142] Hamas [subsequently fired a greater number of rockets into Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Palestinian_rocket_attacks_on_Israel_in_2014) from Gaza, triggering a seven-week-long conflict between the two sides. It was one of the deadliest outbreaks of [open conflict between Israel and the Palestinians](46216.md) in decades. The combination of Palestinian rocket attacks and [Israeli airstrikes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Israeli_strikes_and_Palestinian_casualties_in_the_2014_Israel%E2%80%93Gaza_conflict) resulted in thousands of deaths, the vast majority of which were Gazan Palestinians.[^cite_note-143]&#x20;

### 2018–2019: Great March of Return

Main article: [Great March of Return](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_March_of_Return)



UN OCHA map of the Great March of Return protests, 31 May 2018

From 30 March 2018 to 27 December 2019, [a series of protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018%E2%80%932019_Gaza_border_protests), also known as the Great March of Return, were held each Friday in the Gaza Strip near the [Israel–Gaza barrier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel%E2%80%93Gaza_barrier), during which a total of 223 [Palestinians](23267.md) were killed by Israeli forces.[^cite_note-B'tselem21-144][^cite_note-145] The demonstrators demanded that the [Palestinian refugees](74681.md) must be [allowed to return](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_right_of_return) to lands they were displaced from in what is now Israel. They protested against Israel's [land, air and sea blockade of the Gaza Strip](19224310.md) and the [United States recognition of Jerusalem as capital of Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_recognition_of_Jerusalem_as_capital_of_Israel).[^cite_note-Toameh-146][^cite_note-NY2-147][^cite_note-148]&#x20;

Most of the demonstrators [demonstrated peacefully](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peaceful_demonstration) far from the border fence. Peter Cammack, a fellow with the Middle East Program at the [Carnegie Endowment for International Peace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carnegie_Endowment_for_International_Peace), argued that the march indicated a new trend in Palestinian society and Hamas, with a shift away from violence towards non-violent forms of protest.[^cite_note-149] Some demonstrators were setting tires on fire and launching Molotov cocktails and rocks toward the troops on the opposite side of the border.[^cite_note-nytimes-150][^cite_note-Haaretz30March-151][^cite_note-152] Israeli officials said the demonstrations were used by Hamas as cover for launching attacks against Israel.[^cite_note-jpost1-153]&#x20;



[2018 Gaza border protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Gaza_border_protests), [Bureij](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bureij) refugee camp in Gaza

In late February 2019, a [UN Human Rights Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UN_Human_Rights_Council)'s [independent commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Fact_Finding_Mission_on_the_2018_Gaza_border_protests) found that of the 489 cases of Palestinian deaths or injuries analyzed, only two were possibly justified as responses to danger by Israeli security forces. The commission deemed the rest of the cases illegal, and concluded with a recommendation calling on Israel to examine whether [war crimes](48626.md) or [crimes against humanity](61099.md) had been committed, and if so, to bring those responsible to trial.[^cite_note-154][^cite_note-155]&#x20;

On 28 February 2019, the Commission said it had "'reasonable grounds' to believe Israeli soldiers may have committed war crimes and shot at journalists, health workers and children during protests in Gaza in 2018." Israel refused to take part in the inquiry and rejected the report.[^cite_note-CNN1111-156]&#x20;

### 2021: Israel–Palestine crisis

Before the [2021 Israel–Palestine crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Israel%E2%80%93Palestine_crisis), Gaza had 48% unemployment and half of the population lived in poverty. During the crisis, 66 children died (551 children in the previous conflict). On 13 June 2021, a high level World Bank delegation visited Gaza to witness the damage. Mobilization with UN and EU partners is ongoing to finalize a needs assessment in support of Gaza's reconstruction and recovery.[^cite_note-157]&#x20;

[Another escalation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Gaza%E2%80%93Israel_clashes) between 5 and 8 August 2022 resulted in property damage and displacement of people as a result of airstrikes.[^cite_note-158][^cite_note-159]&#x20;

### 2023–2025: Gaza war

Main articles: [Gaza war](74998519.md), [Outline of the Gaza war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Outline_of_the_Gaza_war), [October 7 attacks](74998317.md), [Israeli invasion of the Gaza Strip](74998519.md), [Proposed Israeli resettlement of the Gaza Strip](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proposed_Israeli_resettlement_of_the_Gaza_Strip), and [Gaza genocide](75668427.md)

See also: [Gaza humanitarian crisis (2023–present)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaza_humanitarian_crisis_\(2023%E2%80%93present\)), [Gaza Strip evacuations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaza_Strip_evacuations), and [Gaza peace plan](81215079.md)



Israeli soldiers in the Gaza Strip on 31 October 2023

On 7 October 2023, the paramilitaries in Gaza, led by the Hamas's [Al-Qassam Brigades](1703333.md), [invaded](74998317.md) southwest Israel, targeting Israeli communities and military bases, killing at least 1,300 people and taking at least 236 hostages.[^cite_note-160] On 9 October 2023, Israel declared war on Hamas and imposed a ["total blockade" of the Gaza Strip](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Israeli_blockade_of_the_Gaza_Strip),[^cite_note-161] with Israeli defense minister [Yoav Gallant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yoav_Gallant) declaring, "There will be no electricity, no food, no fuel, everything is closed. We are fighting human animals and we are acting accordingly."[^cite_note-162][^cite_note-TimesOfIsrael_GazaHostages-163] Gallant changed his position after pressure from US president [Joe Biden](145422.md), and a deal was made on 19 October for Israel and Egypt to allow aid into Gaza.[^cite_note-164]&#x20;



The ruins of Gaza after Israeli airstrikes

After the outbreak of the Gaza war in 2023, there has been a renewed campaign to return Israeli settlers to [Gush Katif](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gush_Katif),[^cite_note-165] including [Hanan Ben Ari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hanan_Ben_Ari) singing "We return to Gush Katif" to Israeli troops.[^cite_note-166] On 19 January 2025, [a ceasefire between Israel and Hamas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/January_2025_Gaza_war_ceasefire) took effect. It lasted until 18 March, when Israel launched a surprise attack.[^cite_note-167]&#x20;

On 5 February 2025, in a joint press conference with Benjamin Netanyahu, US president [Donald Trump](4848272.md) asserted that the US would "[take over the Gaza Strip, we'll own it](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Trump%27s_February_2025_Gaza_Strip_proposal)". The remark sparked discussions regarding US policy in the region.[^cite_note-168][^cite_note-169][^cite_note-170] On 6 February, Trump stated that the proposed transfer of Gaza would happen after the war ended and Gaza's population was relocated.[^cite_note-171]&#x20;

The Gaza war has caused widespread destruction, a [humanitarian crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaza_humanitarian_crisis_\(2023%E2%80%93present\)), and an [ongoing famine](75828824.md) in the Gaza Strip.[^cite_note-Dannenbaum_Dill_2024-23][^cite_note-IPC_22_August_2025-24] Most of the population was [forcibly displaced](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaza_Strip_evacuations).[^cite_note-Dannenbaum_Dill_2024-23] Several human rights organizations such as [Amnesty International](18947898.md) and [B'Tselem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/B%27Tselem), various [genocide studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genocide_studies) and [international law](8195726.md) scholars, and other experts say that [a genocide is taking place in Gaza](75668427.md), although some dispute this.[^cite_note-genocide-25][^cite_note-genocide_int_law_scholars-26] In September 2025 a UN commission of inquiry concluded that there are reasonable grounds to conclude that four of the five genocidal acts defined under international law have been carried by Israel against Palestinians in Gaza.[^cite_note-172] Since the start of the war, over 60,000 Palestinians in Gaza [have been killed](75130785.md), almost half of them [women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women_in_the_Gaza_war#Gaza_Strip) and [children](75987631.md), and more than 148,000 injured.[^cite_note-173][^cite_note-174][^cite_note-175] A study in *[The Lancet](463899.md)* estimated 64,260 deaths in Gaza from [traumatic injuries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traumatic_injuries) by June 2024, while noting a potentially larger death toll when "indirect" deaths are included.[^cite_note-Lancet2025-176][^cite_note-177][^cite_note-178][^cite_note-179] As of May 2025, a comparable figure for traumatic injury deaths would be 93,000 (77,000 to 109,000), representing 4–5% of Gaza's pre-war population.[^cite_note-180]&#x20;

### 2025–present: Gaza peace plan

Main articles: [Gaza peace plan](81215079.md) and [Gaza Strip under Resolution 2803](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaza_Strip_under_Resolution_2803)

In October 2025, the [Gaza peace plan](81215079.md) came into effect, which called for demilitarization and amnesty for Hamas members who disarmed, temporary governance by a Palestinian [technocratic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Technocrat) body overseen by an international commission headed by US president Trump, freedom of residents to depart from and return to Gaza and complete resumption of access to international aid.[^cite_note-181] Under the terms of the plan's first phase, a ceasefire came into effect, all 20 living Israeli hostages were released within 72 hours, around 2000 Palestinian prisoners were released, and the IDF withdrew from 47% of the territory.[^cite_note-182] On 17 November, the United Nations Security Council passed a version of the 20-point peace plan authored by the US, with Russia and China abstaining from the vote.[^cite_note-smithnov2025-183][^cite_note-184] This plan was rejected by Hamas, who objected to the proposed international peacekeeping troops and lack of support for Palestinian self-determination,[^cite_note-185][^cite_note-186] but was accepted by the Palestinian Authority and Israeli prime minister Benjamin Netanyahu.[^cite_note-smithnov2025-183]&#x20;

In addition, financing arrangements have also been discussed. Reports have suggested that the United States has considered using approximately \$5 billion in frozen Palestinian tax revenues, withheld by Israel, to help finance reconstruction efforts in Gaza.[^cite_note-187][^cite_note-188][^cite_note-189]&#x20;

In July 2026, [Hamas](13913.md) announced dissolution of the Gaza government.[^cite_note-190]&#x20;

## Geography



Palestinians on the Gaza beach in 2006



Gaza City in 2018

The Gaza Strip is 41 km (25 mi) long, from 6 to 12 km (3.7 to 7.5 mi) wide, and has a total area of 365 km<sup>2</sup> (141 sq mi).[^cite_note-Arnon-27][^cite_note-CIA_World_Factbook-28] It has a 51 km (32 mi) border with [Israel](9282173.md), and an 11 km (7 mi) border with [Egypt](8087628.md), near the city of [Rafah](584373.md).[^cite_note-191]&#x20;

[Khan Yunis](101330.md) is located 7 km (4.3 mi) northeast of Rafah, and several towns around [Deir el-Balah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deir_el-Balah) are located along the coast between it and [Gaza City](55387.md). [Beit Lahia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beit_Lahia) and [Beit Hanoun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beit_Hanoun) are located to the north and northeast of Gaza City, respectively. The [Gush Katif](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gush_Katif) bloc of Israeli settlements used to exist on the [sand dunes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dune) adjacent to Rafah and Khan Yunis, along the southwestern edge of the 40 km (25 mi) [Mediterranean](19006.md) coastline. Al Deira beach is a popular venue for surfers.[^cite_note-192]&#x20;

The [topography](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Topography) of the Gaza Strip is dominated by three [ridges](714908.md) parallel to the coastline, which consist of [Pleistocene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pleistocene)-[Holocene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holocene) aged [calcareous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calcareous) [aeolian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aeolian_processes) (wind deposited) [sandstones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sandstone), locally referred to as "[kurkar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurkar)", [intercalated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interbedding) with red-coloured [fine grained](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grain_size) [paleosols](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paleosol), referred to as "hamra". The three ridges are separated by [wadis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wadi), which are filled with [alluvial deposits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alluvium).[^cite_note-193] The terrain is flat or rolling, with dunes near the coast. The highest point is Abu 'Awdah (Joz Abu 'Auda), at 105 m (344 ft) above [sea level](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sea_level).&#x20;

The major river in Gaza Strip is [Wadi Gaza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wadi_Gaza), around which the [Wadi Gaza Nature Reserve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wadi_Gaza_Nature_Reserve) was established, to protect the only coastal wetland in the Strip.[^cite_note-meweco-194][^cite_note-195]&#x20;

### Climate

The Gaza Strip has a [hot semi-arid climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semi-arid_climate#Hot_semi-arid_climates) ([Köppen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification) *BSh*), with warm winters during which practically all the annual rainfall occurs, and dry, hot summers. Despite the dryness, humidity is high throughout the year. Annual rainfall is higher than in any part of Egypt at between 225 mm (9 in) in the south and 400 mm (16 in) in the north, but almost all of this falls between November and February.&#x20;

### Environment issues

Environmental problems in Gaza include [desertification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Desertification); [salination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biosalinity) of fresh water; [sewage treatment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sewage_treatment); [water-borne diseases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water-borne_disease); [soil degradation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soil_degradation); and depletion and contamination of underground [water resources](18581463.md). A UN official said in 2024 that "it could take 14 years ... to clear [debris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Construction_waste), including [rubble](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rubble) from destroyed buildings" (of the Gaza war).[^cite_note-Reuters_2024_c119-196]&#x20;

### Gaza Strip under Resolution 2803

See also: [Gaza Strip under Resolution 2803](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaza_Strip_under_Resolution_2803), [Gaza peace plan](81215079.md), and [United Nations Security Council Resolution 2803](81623997.md)

In October 2025, Israel and Hamas accepted the first phase of the [Gaza peace plan](81215079.md) resulting in the cessation of hostilities in the [Gaza War](74998519.md) and a partial Israeli withdrawal from the Gaza Strip. In the second phase of the plan it is envisioned that an [International Stabilization Force](81225292.md) be deployed in the Gaza Strip and a [Board of Peace](81274522.md) and [National Committee for the Administration of Gaza](81600904.md) be established to administer the Gaza Strip for a transitional period, before turning over governance to a reformed [Palestinian Authority](24093.md).[^cite_note-197][^cite_note-198]&#x20;

### Hamas administration

Main article: [Gaza Strip under Hamas](38626632.md)



Gaza Strip territorial control since 10 October 2025



Damaged UN school and remnants of the Ministry of Interior in Gaza City, December 2012

From its takeover of the Gaza Strip in 2007 until July 2026, Hamas exercised executive authority, and it governed through its own *[ad hoc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ad_hoc)* executive, legislative, and judicial bodies.[^cite_note-freedomhouse-199] The Hamas government of 2012 was the second Palestinian Hamas-dominated government, ruling over the Gaza Strip, since the split of the Palestinian National Authority in 2007. It was announced in early September 2012.[^cite_note-hurriyet0209-200] The reshuffle of the previous government was approved by Gaza-based Hamas MPs from the [Palestinian Legislative Council](40067464.md) or parliament.[^cite_note-hurriyet0209-200] Since the Hamas takeover in 2007, the Gaza Strip had been described as a "*de facto* [one-party state](102459.md)", although it tolerated other political groups, including leftist ones such as the [Popular Front for the Liberation of Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Popular_Front_for_the_Liberation_of_Palestine) (PFLP) and the [Democratic Front for the Liberation of Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Front_for_the_Liberation_of_Palestine).[^cite_note-201][^cite_note-rp-14]&#x20;

The legal code Hamas applies in Gaza is based on Ottoman laws, the British Mandate's 1936 legal code, [Palestinian Authority law](18575247.md), [Sharia law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sharia_law), and Israeli military orders. Hamas maintained a judicial system with civilian and military courts and a public prosecution service.[^cite_note-freedomhouse-199][^cite_note-202]&#x20;

Gaza Strip was ranked 6th least electoral [democracy in the Middle East and North Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democracy_in_the_Middle_East_and_North_Africa) according to [V-Dem Democracy indices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V-Dem_Democracy_indices) in 2024 with a score of 0.136 out of one.[^cite_note-report-203]&#x20;

Israel has [occupied large parts of the Gaza Strip](76465951.md) since October 2023 during the [Gaza War](74998519.md). As a result of the war and the October 2025 [peace agreement](81215079.md) that led to the cessation of hostilities, Hamas is in effective control of less than half of the Gaza Strip.&#x20;

On 24 October 2025, Hamas announced it is willing to relinquish control of the Gaza Strip.[^cite_note-204]&#x20;

Security in the parts of the Gaza Strip controlled by Hamas is mainly handled by Hamas's military wing, the [Izz ad-Din al-Qassam Brigades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Izz_ad-Din_al-Qassam_Brigades), internal security service, and civil police force. The Izz ad-Din al-Qassam Brigades had an estimated 30,000 to 50,000 operatives before the 2023 Gaza War.[^cite_note-205]&#x20;

In July 2026, Hamas dissolved its civilian administration in the Gaza Strip as part of a transfer of power to the internationally-recognized [National Committee for the Administration of Gaza](81600904.md).[^cite_note-euronews-1]&#x20;

### Project New Gaza

|  | This section may contain information **not [important or relevant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:What_Wikipedia_is_not#Encyclopedic_content) to the article's subject.** Please help [improve this section](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Gaza_Strip\&action=edit).*&#x20;([Learn how and when to remove this message](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Maintenance_template_removal))* |
| --- | --- |

[Project New Gaza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Project_New_Gaza) is a joint plan led by Husam al-Astal, leader of the [Counter-Terrorism Strike Force](81194590.md) and other anti-Hamas Gazan militias including the [Popular Forces](80146159.md), the [People's Army Northern Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People%27s_Army_Northern_Forces) and the [Popular Defense Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Popular_Defense_Forces_\(Gaza\)).[^cite_note-206][^cite_note-207]&#x20;

Since June 2025, parts of the southeastern Gaza Strip has been under the [administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Popular_Forces_administration_in_the_Gaza_Strip) of the [Popular Forces](80146159.md) led by [Yasser Abu Shabab](80134778.md).&#x20;

Since September 2025, the area around [Qizan an-Najjar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qizan_an-Najjar) has been under the [administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Counter-Terrorism_Strike_Force_administration_in_the_Gaza_Strip) of the [Counter-Terrorism Strike Force](81194590.md) led by Husam al-Astal.&#x20;

### Other groups and ideologies



A rally in support of [Fatah](11104.md) in Gaza City in January 2013

Other Palestinian militant factions operate in the Gaza Strip alongside, and sometimes opposed to Hamas. The [Islamic Jihad Movement in Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Jihad_Movement_in_Palestine), also known as the Palestinian Islamic Jihad (PIJ) is the second largest militant faction operating in the Gaza Strip. Its military wing, the [Al-Quds Brigades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Quds_Brigades), has an estimated 8,000 fighters.[^cite_note-208][^cite_note-IslamicJihad-209]&#x20;

In June 2013, the Islamic Jihad broke ties with Hamas leaders after Hamas police fatally shot the commander of Islamic Jihad's military wing.[^cite_note-IslamicJihad-209] The third largest faction is the [Popular Resistance Committees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Popular_Resistance_Committees). Its military wing is known as the [Al-Nasser Salah al-Deen Brigades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Nasser_Salah_al-Deen_Brigades).&#x20;

Other factions include the [Army of Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Army_of_Islam_\(Gaza_Strip\)) (an Islamist faction of the [Doghmush clan](12081036.md)), the Nidal Al-Amoudi Battalion (an offshoot of the West Bank-based Fatah-linked [al-Aqsa Martyrs' Brigades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Aqsa_Martyrs%27_Brigades)), the [Abu Ali Mustapha Brigades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Ali_Mustapha_Brigades) (armed wing of the PFLP), the [Sheikh Omar Hadid Brigade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheikh_Omar_Hadid_Brigade) ([ISIL](9087364.md) offshoot), Humat al-Aqsa, [Jaysh al-Ummah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaysh_al-Ummah_\(Gaza\)), [Katibat al-Sheikh al-Emireen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Katibat_al-Sheikh_al-Emireen), the Mujahideen Brigades, and the [Abdul al-Qadir al-Husseini Brigades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdul_al-Qadir_al-Husseini_Brigades).[^cite_note-210]&#x20;

Some [Salafi-Jihadis operating in Gaza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamist_anti-Hamas_groups_in_the_Gaza_Strip) have been using as part of their name the term *ʻArḍ al-Ribat* "Land of the [Ribat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ribat)", as a name for Palestine, literally meaning "the land of standing vigilant watch on the frontier", but understood in the context of *global* jihad, which is fundamentally opposed to local, [Palestinian nationalism](47246228.md).[^cite_note-211]&#x20;
